Set within a Chicago Gold Coast penthouse, this living room by designer Alisa Bloom blends vintage elegance with contemporary comfort. A striking 1960s Hans-Agne Jakobsson chandelier takes center stage, casting a warm glow over the room's dynamic design.

Alisa Bloom brings a world of inspiration to her work, gathering furnishings, textiles, and vintage pieces on her travels across the globe and curating them in her sophisticated interiors. Bloom’s eponymous Chicago and New York-based firm offers interior design and construction management services and specializes in whole-home renovations and new construction. Bloom’s interiors blend innovation and practicality, making them perfect for modern families, and the designer is known for her subtle, mood-creating color palettes. The designer and her team work globally and currently have projects underway in the United States, France, and Hong Kong.

Gubi dining chairs mirror a spectacular view of Lake Michigan in a dining room by Amy Kartheiser Design. A BDDW dining table, Ochre chandelier, and Shiir rug complete the space.

Founded in 2014, Amy Kartheiser Design brings a world of influences into its unique interiors. Founder Amy Kartheiser sources inspiration and unique finds from a variety of far-flung destinations, elevating her clients’ spaces with pieces that reflect their personalities. Balancing sophistication and ease, the firm’s work incorporates and highlights the beauty of a home’s setting through natural materials and soothing color palettes. Kartheiser emphasizes wellness in her designs, creating interiors that enhance her clients’ lives. She’s also the founder of Under the Same Sky, a non-profit that benefits the American Foundation for Suicide Prevention. Kartheiser supports the organization through an online store and pop-up shops featuring her global finds.

For the primary bedroom in a lake house, Amy Storm enhanced the relaxing atmosphere with a neutral palette, soft textures, and natural materials.

Designer Amy Storm earned her stripes in hospitality design before founding her namesake residential firm in 2004. Since then, Storm and her team have become known for their knowledgeable approach and ability to tackle projects of all sizes and styles. The firm works with high-end clientele from across the country who are drawn to her signature “Midwest modernish” style, which blends luxury and livability. Based in Glen Ellyn, Amy Storm & Company is a small but mighty team of interior architects and designers with the experience and expertise to lead projects and keep them running smoothly. The company specializes in custom new construction, incorporating interior design from the start of the project to allow the architecture and interiors to feel completely in sync.

The design brief for this Andrea Goldman project was to create interiors that play nice with blue-chip art.

Natural light and organic materials bring a sense of tranquility to the interiors of Andrea Goldman Design. The Glencoe-based firm guides clients through the entire design process while making sure that even the most complex projects remain on track. From a Florida guest compound designed for a repeat client to a beautifully balanced family home in Winnetka, the firm’s portfolio showcases a wide range of project types and styles, though refinement is a throughline in each design.

Ash Street Interiors incorporated a Holly Hunt table and vintage iron furnishings into a bright and airy sunroom.

Founded by Rosemary Wormley in 2015, Ash Street Interiors devises a fresh take on traditional style. The designer and her team specialize in livable luxury, combining elegance and comfort in their tailored, light-filled spaces made for modern families. Elevated materials and polished forms add to the timeless appeal of Wormley’s interiors, which feature tranquil palettes and bespoke accents. The Northfield-based, full-service firm works on residential projects, including whole-home refreshes, renovations, and new construction. Ash Street Interiors’ detail-oriented approach shines in their historical renovations, which preserve the original beauty of the architecture while updating the spaces for 21st-century living.

Interior by Ayya Design House, an interior architecture and design firm based in Chicago.

Palettes in the homes of Ayya Design Haus are built subtly and tonally, through texture and hue. Within founder and creative director Toufic Alayyash's timeless and sophisticated living spaces, moments of contrast have the biggest design voice, creating rooms with emotion.

AZL Interiors devised a casual dining room and reading nook in a 1930s bungalow-style house in Arlington Heights, Illinois. The homeowner, who was passionate about reading and collecting books, needed a space that could welcome her and her family for intimate meals, homework, and reading time.

Even the smallest rooms in the house become standouts in the designs of AZL Interiors. Founder Alessia Zanchi Loffredo highlights the original architecture of a home while cleverly adapting residences for modern life, incorporating materials that will develop a rich patina over time. The resulting interiors feel rooted in history yet functional for 21st-century families. The Glen Ellyn–based firm specializes in large-scale renovations and custom homes, designing with a special focus on sustainability, comfort, and durability. AZL Interiors collaborates with a talented roster of architects, contractors, and artisans to bring each project to life with impeccable attention to detail.

Going off the “funky, adult lounge” directive from their clients, Bishop created a cozy jewel box space in a Naperville home with cozy fabrics, pink-hued patterns, and eye-catching stone.

Lively patterns, playful color palettes, and dynamic art add energy and individuality to Bishop Studio’s modern, livable interiors. Led by founding principal Jennie Bishop, the Chicago-based firm focuses on showcasing the personalities of its clients within luxurious yet comfortable spaces, from a contemporary Palm Springs retreat to an art-filled Tribeca live/work loft. Bishop Studio brings its signature spirited style to residential and boutique hospitality projects across the country.

Brooke Lang Design drew inspiration from the breathtaking views of the Chicago skyline for this condo at One Bennett Park. In the living room, the firm blended a playful yet sophisticated color palette, using saturated hues and varied textures to enhance the depth and richness of the design.

Brooke Lang Design blends timeless style with unexpected surprises, creating elegant yet eclectic interiors. Founded by architect and designer Brooke Lang in 2012, the interior design and project management firm draws inspiration from classic Parisian architecture and modern style, bringing in rich color palettes, striking wallpaper, and luxe textiles into their residential, commercial, and hospitality projects. Brooke Lang Design offers a range of services, from turn-key interior design for rooms or entire homes to one-day consultations, and has completed projects across the country, including spaces in California, Kentucky, New York, and Georgia.

Designed to tell homeowners' personal stories, Bruce Fox Design interiors are welcome hosts to the collected objects that represent lives well-lived. Context, taste, and functionality build the rest of the warm, inviting rooms where new memories are made.

In this Chicago penthouse, Brynn Olson opted for tailored yet comfortable pieces that don’t distract from the stunning city views. Curvaceous forms and custom ivory drapery temper the room’s more masculine elements, including custom sectionals clad in gray John Rosselli and Associates wool fabric, a marble and iron credenza by Alfonso Marina, and a luxe leather-and-iron stool.

After a successful stint at Nate Berkus Associates, designer Brynn Olson struck out on her own, founding her namesake firm in 2012. The designer’s mantra, “how you shape your space will shape your day,” is at the heart of the firm’s mission and design process, and Olson and her team work to devise personalized spaces that feel timeless and livable. Brynn Olson Design Group works with residential and commercial clients in Chicago and beyond. The firm works in a variety of styles, designing everything from a glam Chicago penthouse to a modern farmhouse-style home in Hinsdale. But no matter the style, each space is beautifully layered with texture, unique art, and one-of-a-kind elements.

Modernist design influences bring style and warmth to Candace Mary Interiors' work while detail-driven millwork, pops of jewel tone color, and playful deployment of bold tile add contemporary function and everyday joy.

Centered by Design transformed the sunroom of a David Adler–designed property in Illinois for the House Beautiful Whole Home 2023. Claire Staszak incorporated yellow trellises, cheerful prints, and blue-and-white stripes in the spirited space.

Claire Staszak designs spaces with soul, putting a modern, eclectic spin on classic design. The Centered by Design founder and her team are particularly known for crafting timeless, functional kitchens featuring their in-house line of custom cabinetry. Staszak’s love of historic design is evident in her interiors, which feel fresh yet true to their setting, whether it’s a charming 1930s colonial or a regal prewar residence. The firm is skilled at working closely with architects, builders, and landscape designers, making the process simple for its clients. Centered by Design offers everything from full-service interior design to virtual consultations, making its expertise available to homeowners with a wide range of project sizes and budgets.

Designed for a family of avid readers, the leather-clad entry—a nod to leather-bound books—with its gilt ceiling and roaring fire provides a warm welcome home to this East Lake Shore Drive apartment in Chicago by Craig & Company. The leather walls were made by Michele Costello of CSI Designs.

From chic urban penthouses to gracious country homes, Craig & Company brings a sense of elegance to interiors across the country. Led by founder Joan Craig, the firm is based in Chicago, New York, and Sagaponack, and serves discerning clients across the country. Known for character-filled spaces, Craig and her team look at each project holistically, making sure all aspects of the design, including architecture and art, work together harmoniously. Craig & Company frequently collaborates with artists and artisans to add dazzling details, such as gilded ceilings or sculptural lighting, to make the firm’s spaces sing. The versatile firm takes on an array of project types, and recent examples include a private Chicago club, a family compound, and a Hamptons barn.

In a kitchen by Dawn Reeves Design, sculptural lighting brings bold appeal, while varied cabinet finishes create harmony.

Whether designing streamlined, modern spaces or elegant, traditional homes, Dawn Reeves Design knows how to make the small details shine. Based in the Chicago suburbs, the firm works on a wide range of projects, including historic renovations and new builds, and is known for its bespoke touches. Dawn Reeves Design is able to render 3D walkthroughs, which help clients virtually experience the finished space (and feel confident in their choices).

Deborah Lund Interiors designed a Chicago family’s dining room to be the perfect space for elegant dinners, group homework sessions, and a comfortable place to watch the city’s spectacular sunrises.

Art plays a major part in the sophisticated designs of Deborah Lund Interiors. Longtime collector and principal designer Deborah Lund curates an exceptional mix of contemporary art, midcentury pieces, and vintage lighting in her interiors, blending styles and historical influences for a collected look. Curvaceous furnishings upholstered in tactile fabrics and distinctive wall treatments bring comfort and individuality to spaces, and Lund adds elements of whimsy, such as an ombre stair runner or playful lamps. The firm works with clients across the country and has completed projects from Palm Springs to Miami.

A light-filled kitchen designed for gathering, where refined details support everyday living and entertaining.

Family-specific design solutions assure that every home by Drury Design is functional and personalized. The woman-owned, award-winning design-build firm brings traditional interior styles into a new era creates timeless aesthetic appeal.

Layers of texture and pattern come together for a major impact in a dining room by designer Elizabeth Krueger.

Elizabeth Krueger can do everything from light and airy to dark and drama-filled in her captivating spaces. The Chicago designer works with each client to determine the purpose behind their home and translate it into highly personal and meaningful environments. Elizabeth Krueger Design was founded in 2012, and over the past 12 years, the firm has established itself as a go-to for everything from Chicago penthouses and historic Highland Park homes to Michigan farmhouses and Florida retreats. The firm works on projects of all scales, including renovations and new builds, as well as furnishing, organizing, and refreshing projects.

In this custom kitchen, Elizabeth Mollen Interiors had the cabinets finished in Farrow & Ball De Nimes and worked with Porter Teleo to create a custom hand-painted wall covering to match the cabinetry.

Elizabeth Mollen began her career working in the fashion industry in New York and Los Angeles, and in 2012, Mollen shifted to interiors. Mollen and her team bring a sense of fun to their spaces, incorporating vivid wall coverings and textiles, many of which are designed by the firm. Stone Textile Studio works on projects in the Chicago area and across the country, helping clients to infuse personality into their homes. Locals can also shop the firm’s collections of rugs, wallpaper, and textiles, as well as a curated mix of art and vintage pieces, at their Highland Park store, the Vault Creative.

For Emily Sturgess, paying homage to the history and character of a 1930s Long Island home was essential—but being that its owners were a young family, the living room’s sofas and chairs were covered in Perennials performance fabrics to ensure the room can take on anything. Antiques and vintage pieces were mixed in to ground the expansive space and give it gravitas.

Traditional design is updated for modern living in the work of Emily Sturgess. The designer’s eye for beauty and love of historic homes is evident in her timeless spaces, which are enhanced by gracious wall coverings, elegant antiques, and sophisticated (but comfortable!) seating. Her Winnetka firm, Emily Sturgess Design, works with a talented roster of artisans and vendors who share its commitment to creating spaces that clients are proud to show off. Sturgess and her team take on projects nationwide and are currently designing interiors in California, Florida, and New York, in addition to their local projects.

Bespoke cabinetry, finished in a custom color created in partnership with O’Brien Harris Cabinetry, acts as the framework for this striking kitchen by Goesling Group. Champagne marble tops, natural walnut and unlacquered brass accents, dramatic lighting, and a vintage 19th-century fruitwood table round out the space.

Sisters Sarah and Rebecca Goesling bring design and construction know-how to their Park Ridge–based firm, Goesling Group. The multidisciplinary design collective specializes in luxury interior design, CMF design, and trend forecasting, ensuring that each project of theirs is distinctive. Whether crafting a residential interior or designing a brand identity, Goesling Group approaches each design with curiosity and creativity, resulting in projects that are cutting-edge while still being tailored to each client.

Located in Miami’s The Surf Club, a dining room designed by House of Hunt features a statement-making blue dining table by Based Upon, echoing the ocean views outdoors. An Alison Berger Glassworks light fixture hangs above.

After leading her legendary luxury design brand for over 30 years, Holly Hunt founded House of Hunt in 2021, allowing the designer the opportunity to lend her impeccable taste and wealth of knowledge to interior architecture and design projects. The firm’s projects showcase a modern, streamlined sensibility without sacrificing comfort. House of Hunt’s neutral interiors offer a sophisticated backdrop for blue chip art and one of a kind design elements, and the boutique studio’s refined pieces allow high-quality materials to shine. Hunt continues to conceive bespoke furnishings for her projects and collaborations, including a collection of striking stone and metal mantels for Marmi.

House of Mondi enhanced vintage 1920s style with elevated finishes and modern conveniences in a Chicago kitchen. Lacquered eucalyptus cabinetry in a high gloss finish offers richness and warmth, while glass and polished nickel cabinets visually open up the space. Statement quartzite countertops were chosen to highlight an oversized bullnose ledge, a nod to European kitchen designs.

Founded in 2001, House of Mondi prides itself on creating spaces that tell a story, evoke emotions, and improve the lives of its occupants. Design principal Donna Mondi’s signature style blends luxurious details with rich textures and sculptural accents, and the designer makes it a priority to honor each home’s context and history. This attention to detail results in interiors that feel right at home in their setting, whether it’s a penthouse or a rambling estate. The full-service firm specializes in residential interiors and serves clients nationwide from its offices in Chicago and Denver. In addition to her dazzling spaces, Mondi has also lent her expert eye to collections of bespoke furnishings and stone fireplaces.

Metalic accents, including a custom hood, and a subdued palette give a Chicago kitchen by Ilene April Interiors timeless polish.

A home’s existing architecture and her clients’ personal treasures inspire designer Ilene April’s elegant interiors. The designer’s Highland Park–based firm keeps functionality top of mind throughout a project, resulting in spaces that are livable and beautiful. Ilene April Interiors mixes contemporary and traditional elements throughout its designs, making sure the furnishings, fabrics, and fixtures she selects will stand the test of time. April also designs custom furnishings, collaborating with artisans in Chicago, New York, and LA to add one-of-a-kind elements to her projects. The firm works on projects of all sizes and budgets, assisting clients with everything from whole-home designs to color consultations to pick the perfect palette.

In this Lincoln Park row house’s front parlor, Imparfait Design Studio kept it classic—with a modern twist. A traditional tufted sofa from Jayson Home and a vintage glass coffee table are mixed with a Stark ikat rug and midcentury-modern-inspired blue barrel chairs.

Imparfait Design Studio founders Rebekah Zaveloff and Nick Nichols met while working in the restaurant industry, so it’s no surprise that hospitality is at the heart of their interiors. The residential design firm prides itself on devising spaces that people never want to leave, incorporating vintage pieces, plush seating, and eclectic accents that feel collected over time. The firm has offices in Chicago and Lakeside, Michigan, and works extensively in Miami—from Coconut Grove beach houses to polished Gold Coast condos—and throughout the country.  Imparfait Design Studio’s sister firm, KitchenLab, creates home kitchens that are worthy of a Michelin-starred chef (or just a passionate entertainer).

Deep green cabinets and brass hardware add richness and charm to this hardworking butler’s pantry from Interiors by Jen Davidson.

Designer Jen Davidson creates homes that are made to be lived in and enjoyed, brimming with personal touches and heirlooms that reflect the client’s lifestyle and story. The designer’s Glenview-based firm, Interiors by Jen Davidson, is known for its use of natural materials, architectural details, and timeless furnishings in warm, comfortable spaces. Davidson and her team work with clients across the country on full-service interior design projects, renovations, and new builds.

J'Miracle Designs transformed a once bland Chicago dining room into a transitional modern look. The firm made a statement in the space by fully covering the confined space with patterned wall coverings, brass accessories, and multitextured chairs.

Touches of drama set the mood in the effortlessly luxurious spaces created by Jasmine Artis and her firm, J'Miracle Designs. Working in a wide range of styles, from Art Deco–inspired to contemporary, the firm brings out the homeowner’s personality with statement lighting, metallic accents, and comfortable yet elegant furnishings. Artis draws on her corporate background to give clients a transparent and stress-free process, whether they’re collaborating on a single room refresh or a whole home redesign. The firm also works with homeowners to create stylish yet functional rental properties and helps commercial clients devise offices, hotels, and retail spaces that speak for their brand.

What was originally intended to be the dining room in this stately Gold Coast townhome was reimagined as a Parisian-styled salon by James Thomas. A striking navy mohair banquette extends the length of one wall and offers a chic spot for a cocktail or auxiliary dining for small groups. The neutral walls are the perfect backdrop for the client’s impressive contemporary art collection. The Paul Ferrante center pendant provides a sculptural element that balances perfectly with the graphic art pieces.

Founded in 2003 by designers Tom Riker and James Dolenc, James Thomas specializes in “effortless elegance,” infusing their projects with a sense of sophistication and ease. The duo’s impactful spaces blend carefully curated color palettes, a mix of classic and contemporary furnishings, and plenty of pieces with character. They’re not afraid to take big swings with impactful details, such as a latticed wall treatment or faux tortoiseshell ceiling, to add a wow factor. With over 400 projects completed, James Thomas’ team of 16 has the design process down to a science. The firm serves clients in the Chicago area and across the country, from Beverly Hills to Park City to Palm Beach.

In a Jasmin Reese Interiors project in Coeur D'Alene, Idaho, a primary bath features a custom walk-in shower with lilac marble, a brass English tub, and a custom vanity with an antique mirror.

Color and pattern are the stars of Jasmin Reese’s lively spaces. Ceilings pop with eye-catching wallpaper, rooms are furnished with a well-edited selection of unique pieces, and walls are drenched in color and accented with impactful art. The designer isn’t afraid to mix prints or periods, and as a result, her bold spaces feel collected and one of a kind. Reese, who founded her eponymous firm over 10 years ago, brings a playful approach to interior design, as evidenced by the comic book that introduces potential clients to Jasmin Reese Interiors’ process.

A chic primary bedroom by Jenami Designs evokes the feel of a luxurious boutique hotel—elevated, moody, and effortlessly sophisticated.

The client is the driving force and inspiration behind each interior created by Jenami Designs. Led by designer Jennifer Kole, the Chicago-based firm prides itself on both its highly personalized approach and its ability to translate the client’s taste and lifestyle into exciting, comfortable spaces. Mixing a wide range of influences, the firm’s layered interiors bring together vintage and modern furnishings alongside rich hues, architectural details, and eye-catching art.

The warm, well-proportioned, and well-collected interiors of Jodi Morton Design take the homes' architecture and owners as primary sources of inspiration. Designs are eclectic yet functional, with an inspired use of bold color.

In a traditional Lincoln Park Georgian home designed by BBA Architects, JP Interiors brought in a layered mix of antiques and modern pieces to sit in the backdrop of the room's focal point: a carved marble mantle and antique mirror fireplace wall.

Elegance and charm grace each space that Chicago-based JP Interiors creates. For nearly 35 years, the firm has been a go-to for clients seeking classic, comfortable spaces elevated with a curated mix of antiques and bespoke pieces. Specializing in residential renovations and interior decoration, the team, which includes founders Blaine Johnson and Karen Pearson and design director Haley Pearson, has worked on projects in 15 states, from oceanfront retreats in Nantucket to historic family homes in the Windy City.

The great room of this desert home by Kadlec Architecture + Design opens up to the pool and mountains beyond.

Kadlec Architecture + Design was founded by Steve Kadlec in 2004 to convene a tightly knit team of designers with exceptional experience. At 10 people, the studio remains deliberately small so that they can work closely to give each project their full and focused attention. The Kadlec team has worked on “quietly confident and curated spaces” with growing families, empty nesters, young professionals, and established executives, shopkeepers, and developers. While each client relationship is its own, every project is connected by a thoughtful, disciplined, and detailed approach.

In designer Kate Marker’s Barrington, Illinois, home, a space is accented with checkered floors by Francois and Co., wallpaper and paint by The Little Greene Paint Company, and accessories from Kate Marker Home.

Barrington-based Kate Marker Interiors doesn’t just design homes; they create sanctuaries. Natural materials, soft textiles, abundant light, and nods to the great outdoors bring a sense of comfort and easy-going polish, while touches of whimsy add charm to each residence. Whether building homes from the ground up, working on renovations, or designing interior projects, founder Kate Marker and her team are known for expertly curated spaces that feel personal, functional, and welcoming.

Sometimes Kim Scodro’s interiors are soft and subtle, while other times, they’re packed with statements like a giant fabric bow mounted on a dressing room ceiling, a dining room decorated in tree motifs, or a glossy green TV room. Either way, the spaces this Chicago-based firm creates are elegant and refined.

To create elegant home interiors like those of Kristen Ekeland Interiors requires a keen understanding of how the interactions between texture, sunlight, and color can transform a room's design into something magical at every time of day.

In this stunning 1920s residence renovated by designer Lauren Ashley Allan, modernity meets soul, spirit, and character, crafting a space that honors artful living. Throughout, the home combines eclectic elements and luxurious comfort, offering a tactile blend of styles that feel both sophisticated and grounded.

After earning her stripes working with AD100 designers like Nate Berkus and Kelly Wearsler, Lauren Ashley Allan struck out on her own in 2020, launching her eponymous Hinsdale-based firm. Allen has an eye for timeless and luxurious materials, incorporating elements of marble, brass, and wood that will grow even more beautiful with age. The designer and her team juxtapose styles and periods within their elegant interiors, drawing inspiration from history and their clients’ lives. The firm offers full-service interior design to residential and commercial clients around the world, working on everything from historic renovations to boutique hotels to yacht design projects.

Working with Michael Hershenson Architect and Tartan Builders, Lauren Coburn brought a modern, luxurious aesthetic to a new construction project for a young family in River Forest, Illinois.

After earning her stripes at Tigerman McCurry Architects, Chicago-based designer Lauren Coburn founded her eponymous firm in 2004, bringing an expertise in interior architecture to her design practice. The designer and her team tackle renovations, new construction, and decorating projects, bringing out their clients’ unique visions and collaborating closely with architects and builders to ensure that it comes to life. Whether designing a traditional residence or a modern passive house, the firm keeps functionality and elegance at the heart of each design.

Leah Bolger Design brought together soft neutral hues, lovely textures, and touches of black from the vintage Steinway and objects to capture the client’s aesthetic and spirit in this sophisticated living room.

Designer Leah Bolger brings over 25 years of experience to her eponymous Glenview-based luxury interiors firm. Her background as a college instructor of interior architecture and graduate studies is evident in her well-considered spaces, which showcase her deep knowledge of architecture and interiors. Bolger enlivens her traditional interiors with contemporary elements, and functionality is always a focus. The firm’s work encompasses residential and commercial projects in Chicago and beyond, including new construction and renovations. The studio offers a range of services, including interior architecture, in-house kitchen and bath design, and space planning.

Designed for two busy parents who wanted a calming place to unwind, Lindsey Goddard Interiors layered different textures to bring warmth and depth to a Lincoln Park primary bedroom while maintaining a completely neutral palette.

Lindsey Goddard Interiors updates warm, classic stylistic practices with a contemporary perspective, resulting in elegant, beautifully balanced residences. The Chicago-based firm offers a boutique experience with the resources and expertise of a larger studio, thanks to its multidisciplinary team. Lindsey Goddard Interiors works across the United States and abroad, guiding clients and keeping each aspect of the project on track through close collaboration and plenty of creativity.

M + M Interior Design designed this pattern-reading room for the Kips Bay Decorator Show House in Dallas.

Sisters Leslie Martin and Kim Meardon are the Ms behind M + M Interior Design. The Kenilworth-based firm is known for its fresh and functional traditional interiors, bringing in an eclectic mix of vintage and antique pieces, eye-catching patterns, and impactful wall treatments. Whether they’re designing a lakeside retreat or a classic brownstone, Martin and Meardon are able to incorporate show-stopping moments into any setting. M + M Interior Design collaborates with clients across the county and is experienced in everything from historic renovations to new builds. These Chicago interior designers rely on their time-tested and efficient process to make each project run smoothly.

While bold color is certainly a strength for M2 Design Lab, expressive designs in any palette are a given. Each home is conceived as an artistic gesture, rendering spaces as unique as their clients' tastes, needs, and location.

For a client’s summer getaway in Union Pier, Michigan, Mia Rao Design leaned into a water and sky–inspired palette with Dellarobbia sofas and Geiger chairs.

Chicago’s Mia Rao Design puts a modern twist on classic design, working with a given home’s preexisting architecture and the client’s personal style to devise one-of-a-kind interiors. Founder and principal Mia Rao and senior designer Robyn Swanson work on a wide variety of homes with their team. From historic homes to lakeside retreats, they collaborate closely with architects and contractors while guiding homeowners through the entire process.

Nate Berkus Associates conjured a cozy primary bedroom in a Cambridge, Massachusetts, home, incorporating a mix of vintage and bespoke items, including a custom suede headboard and bed, an Angelo Mangiarotti marble console, Jacques Quinet brass table lamps, a Louis Phillipe mirror and a Louis XVI bench.

One of the most influential figures in American design, Nate Berkus continues to innovate and set trends through his elevated, inviting interiors. Led by Berkus and partner Lauren Buxbaum Gordon, Nate Berkus Associates operates from offices in Chicago, New York, and Los Angeles, and works on residential and commercial projects around the world. From penthouses to lakeside retreats, Berkus and Gordon craft homes that feel luxurious and are designed to evolve over the years.

In a River North condo designed by Nicholas Moriarty Interiors, a Robert Motherwell painting takes center stage in the living area, which features a trio of Boffi sofas. In the adjacent dining space, Moriarty placed a Flos pendant light above a vintage table and chairs.

Every homeowner is different, so Nicholas Moriarty Interiors tailors the design process to each client and the way they think. Each project begins with a deep dive into the client’s lifestyle and story through written and verbal questionnaires and “visual listening activities”, resulting in expertly curated and personality-packed interiors. The multi-disciplinary firm focuses on full-scale interior design and renovation projects and offers everything from interior architectural consulting and space planning to art curation and custom furniture and casegood design. Since it was founded in 2008, Nicholas Moriarty Interiors has become known for its artful, deeply considered designs and counts celebrities, Fortune 500 execs, and artists among its clients and fans.

For a project on Chicago's North Shore, North Shore Nest helped their client make her dark home office something special. Designers Melinda Cahill and Suzanne Glavin coated the wood paneling with high-gloss blue paint and brought in layers of blues, pinks, neutrals, and raspberry tones for an overall light and airy feel.

In 2004, Melinda Cahill and Suzanne Glavin teamed up to found North Shore Nest, their Glenview-based residential interior design firm. The design duo and their team specialize in warm, inviting spaces for families and young professionals, imbuing rooms with color, pattern, and a palpable sense of fun. North Shore Nest offers full-service interior design as well as full-day or hour-long consultations. Clients have a dedicated concierge to keep them informed with frequent updates throughout the design process. The firm works on projects in the Chicago area, as well as with clients in Lake Geneva, Wisconsin, and has recently expanded its reach with new construction projects in Arizona and Florida.

There’s no shortage of storage in this River North kitchen, which Outline Interiors designed with the client's love for cooking and entertaining in mind.

#5109. Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday after the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank left roughly three million customers unable to access their accounts, turning a banking failure into a political emergency.

The announcement did not end the confrontation. Paraguay’s lower house voted 78-0 to impeach Peña minutes later, sending the charges to the Senate as Vice President Pedro Alliana prepared to take over, per AP.

The bank’s shutdown has also revived an argument familiar to Bitcoin holders: Money held by a financial intermediary is only as accessible as that institution allows. Self-custodied Bitcoin works differently, although it comes with risks of its own.

As the Bitcoin OGs would say, “Bitcoin fixes this.”

Paraguay’s central bank placed Ueno into resolution after regulators found its solvency ratio had fallen below half the legal minimum. The process, announced on November 3, followed accounting adjustments to the bank’s financial statements and left its app, cards, and transfers out of service.

Ueno claimed around 2.9 million customers, more than 60% of Paraguay’s adults. It also held roughly $800 million in public-sector deposits, including pension and health funds, according to AP, complicating an already painful freeze on ordinary savings and wages.

Peña previously held a stake in a company linked to Ueno’s owners, a relationship that fueled allegations of conflicts of interest. He has denied wrongdoing and said he sold his interest in March 2025, according to Reuters.

“My greatest wish is that the return of those funds be guaranteed, that the Deposit Guarantee Fund fulfill its purpose,” Peña said in his resignation address. The Deposit Guarantee Fund would be the equivalent of the FDIC in the United States.

Mensaje del Presidente de la República, Santiago Peña, al pueblo paraguayo. pic.twitter.com/RBz6IfFV9L

— Santiago Peña (@SantiPenap) November 5, 2026

Outside Congress, the anger was less diplomatic. “Santiago Peña and the Colorado Party have stolen all our savings,” protester Luis Pineda told AP. “We are fed up with 70 years of plunder.”

The central bank says Ueno’s failure is an isolated case, not evidence of a broader banking collapse. It has announced a deposit guarantee of up to 228.3 million guaraníes per depositor, roughly $36,000, with initial payments targeting eligible accounts holding no more than 10 million guaraníes.

Bitcoin was born during another banking crisis. Its first block, mined in January 2009, included a newspaper headline about a second bailout for British banks, a historical detail long cited by supporters who distrust reliance on financial intermediaries.

A Bitcoin holder who controls a wallet’s private keys can authorize transfers from the wallet without asking a third party, like a bank, to unlock an account. That is the principle behind the crypto saying “not your keys, not your coins,” and the distinction from leaving Bitcoin on an exchange that can itself freeze withdrawals.

But Bitcoin is not perfect. Self-custody has its own risks: Forget your keys or lose them and there is no way you can recover your funds, there is nobody who can assist you, and no law enforcement agency who can do anything for you.

Paraguay’s central bank said Thursday its first guarantee payments would go to depositors with up to 10 million guaraníes, no Ueno debts and an account at another financial institution.

#5107. Strive’s bitcoin gains drive $509 million Q3 profit as BTC buying ramps up

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Strive added 15,124 BTC in October and ended the month with $490.4 million in cash as it targets second place among holders.

Bitcoin treasury company Strive reported $509 million in third-quarter net income on Friday, entirely driven by paper gains on its BTC holdings.

Those unrealized bitcoin gains topped $533.7 million for the quarter, while diluted earnings came out to $5.24 per share. Strive also reported roughly $2.7 million in revenue, mostly from investment advisory fees and the medical device business it acquired through its January takeover of Semler Scientific.

Strive bought 8,136 BTC during the quarter to end the quarter with 28,000 BTC. It then scooped up another 15,124 BTC in October alone, bringing its current holdings to 43,124 BTC worth roughly $3.25 billion.

Strive controls the fourth-largest bitcoin treasury among publicly listed companies behind Strategy, Metaplanet and Twenty One.

During a podcast appearance in early September, CEO Matt Cole said it would not be "out of the realm of possibility" to end the year as the number two largest bitcoin holder. It's likely that this could become a reality before we even reach December. Strive now needs just 876 more BTC to reach the number two spot.

Strive reported an 18.5% "bitcoin yield" for the quarter, its metric for growth in bitcoin holdings compared to its outstanding shares.

"Bitcoin is the hurdle rate at Strive," Cole wrote on X following the earnings release, arguing that growth in bitcoin per share has been the biggest driver of ASST's more than 100% outperformance against bitcoin this year.

Cole also pointed to the company's SATA and SATS preferred shares to help bring in returns above bitcoin for shareholders. Strive raised around $609 million in proceeds from its SATS offering in October and later launched another $1 billion program to sell more SATS shares in the future.

Strive held a little over $490 million in cash and equivalents as well as roughly $50.1 million in Strategy's own STRC preferred shares.

Despite the positive quarterly report, ASST shares were down about 2% to $20.20 in the first few hours of trading on Friday, extending their decline over the past 30 days to more than 31%. Bitcoin fell roughly 12% over the same period and was trading around $75,600.

#5106. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.47 Million in Crypto From Investigations

A former FBI agent pleaded guilty Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency with wallet credentials the bureau had collected in its own cases, the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the Eastern District of Virginia announced.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, entered pleas to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. U.S. District Judge Patricia Tolliver Giles accepted the plea. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled for sentencing on March 4, 2027.

Court papers put the illicit haul at $2,474,380.66. Prosecutors say Yaroch made roughly 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials tied to 35 separate FBI investigations, drawing on wallets controlled by investigation subjects, adversarial nation-state actors and at least one wallet the FBI itself had created for an operation.

The Justice Department Office of the Inspector General had flagged the case in August after Yaroch’s arrest on a criminal complaint. That earlier account described a smaller set of transfers — about 10 moves totaling roughly $1 million — and noted that Yaroch self-reported the activity to two department employees on July 29, 2026. The plea figures are larger.

From 2017 through early 2025, Yaroch worked in the FBI Boston Division on a squad focused on an adversarial nation-state. From February 2025 through July 31, 2026, he served in the FBI’s Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at headquarters, with access to systems that stored cryptocurrency credentials gathered in investigations.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch used that access to move coins out of those wallets and into ones he controlled. After the transfers, he swapped the stolen cryptocurrency into other digital assets and ran further transactions meant to obscure its source, nature and ownership.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, he moved about $93,082 into one of his accounts, then shuffled funds among other accounts to cover debts, including credit cards, car payments and loans, the office said.

FOX 5 DC, citing the same U.S. Attorney’s Office release, noted the Portugal planning that appears in the plea papers. Yaroch asked ChatGPT how to maximize investment returns and where to live in the European Union. After the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers a power of attorney on June 15, 2026, to register him with the Portuguese Tax and Customs Authority, obtain a tax identification number and collect the password to his finance portal. He arranged travel to Portugal for September 2026.

The agreed statement of facts goes beyond the dollar figure. Yaroch’s conduct compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, the office said, and it compromised FBI sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection and criminal cases. It also “deprived FBI employees of their ability to carry out their functions to protect public safety and national security.”

First Assistant U.S. Attorney Theophani K. Stamos and Daniel S. Wierzbicki, special agent in charge of the FBI Washington Field Office’s Counterintelligence Division, announced the plea. Assistant U.S. Attorneys Kathleen Robeson and Russell Carlberg are prosecuting, with help from the National Security Cyber Section of the Justice Department’s National Security Division and the DOJ inspector general’s Mid-Atlantic region. The case is 1:26-CR-208 in the Eastern District of Virginia.

The admission lands in a week when federal crypto-crime stories keep stacking up — from a failed bitcoin kidnapping plot that produced its own guilty plea last month to FBI tallies putting U.S. crypto fraud losses above $11 billion in 2025, a figure the White House has used to frame a wider crackdown.

What sentencing will do with the gap between Yaroch’s July self-report and the $2.47 million plea figure is still open. So is how many of the 35 FBI cases can be repaired after an insider treated the bureau’s own wallet credentials as a personal ATM. March 4, 2027, is when Judge Giles gets to answer the first question.

#5104. Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Anthropic launched a personal finance feature for Claude on Thursday, letting users connect bank accounts and ask the chatbot what they have spent, what bills are due, and how much money they have left.

The announcement came with an accusation from crypto investor Anthony Pompliano, who says Anthropic tried to get inside information from his competing startup, Silvia, months before the rollout.

Claude can then summarize spending, spot duplicate charges or unexpected fees, explain interest payments, and build charts or bill calendars. Anthropic's example shows a user asking where their money went this month, then following up to find out what remains before payday.

"Access is read-only, and Claude can't move, manage, or invest your money," Anthropic wrote on X. "Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations," the company added.

The assistant can see balances, credit limits, account names and types, the last four digits of account numbers, and up to 24 months of transactions from accounts users approve. According to Anthropic's documentation, conversations inside the Personal Finance hub are not used to train its models, but account balances may lag and AI-generated figures should be checked against bank records.

Also, how Anthropic tracks its users’ financial data and uses it beyond model training is not completely clear—and this is extremely valuable information for big data companies.

Pompliano, whose Silvia product bills itself as a personal AI chief financial officer, posted a lengthy complaint on X shortly after Claude's announcement. He alleged that Anthropic personnel began approaching Silvia engineers in April, supposedly because they were interested in the startup's growth and computing needs.

"Anthropic was fishing for details on how we built our product and why it was so popular," he wrote. Pompliano also described an alleged steak dinner involving Anthropic representatives, claiming someone told the Silvia team that the meal was on CEO Dario Amodei's tab.

His engineers declined to divulge sensitive information, he claimed, adding that someone at Anthropic warned his team about the upcoming launch days earlier.

"Today, Anthropic launched a Claude wrapper that is masquerading as a personal finance product," Pompliano wrote. He also claimed Silvia's computing costs are roughly 97% lower when using its own models and hardware instead of frontier AI models, which most likely mean they are using a local model, likely finetuned for the startup’s specific purpose.

That said, Anthropic was not the first general-purpose chatbot maker to move into personal finance, either. OpenAI launched similar bank connections for ChatGPT in May, also using Plaid for read-only access to financial information, and Agents like Hermes, Open Claw, or Grok Bot can also do pretty similar tasks given the proper skills, permissions, and instructions.

Anthropic says Personal Finance is a first step and is exploring partnerships with licensed professionals and regulated financial firms. For now, the beta is restricted to U.S. customers on three paid Claude plans.

#5102. This 322-Square-Foot Apartment Gives Classic Style a Contemporary Update

Some gifts are best kept a surprise until the moment they’re handed over to their recipient. That was the case with this small 322-square-foot apartment located in the Vidny Gorod residential complex, about four miles from Moscow, which the owner, Lara, received as a gift from her daughter, Marta, and her son-in-law. “The couple personally oversaw the renovation, keeping the project a secret until the very end,” says interior designer Polina Chumakova, founder of Lagolga Buro, who oversaw the design. “Lara knew nothing about it and didn’t see the space that they were going to give her until it was completed and she first set foot in her new home.”

In the living room, the custom cabinet by Avantiz complements a vintage table and chairs by Tok. On the tabletop, an arrangement of vintage vases by Mak.buro adds a decorative touch.

Lara’s passion for cooking and baking guided some of the design choices, as her daughter envisioned turning the 322-square-foot apartment into a home capable of welcoming the entire family. “It was important to create a comfortable space not only for cooking but also for spending time together, so that on weekends the apartment could be filled with the aroma of homemade desserts,” explains Chumakova. “Lara has two children—Marta and her brother—and two grandchildren, ages 13 and 9. The family often gets together on weekends and at the holidays.”

The apartment’s contemporary-meets-classic design reflects Vidny Gorod’s architecture and its urban context. “The style was carefully defined, choosing solutions suited to both the home’s size and the budget for this project,” says Chumakova. “We invested in high-quality finishes and carefully selected furniture, lighting, and accessories. The idea was to combine a contemporary, understated design with natural walnut accents and a few vintage details. The result is an interior with a classic character, yet without references to any specific era.”

The wall of the living space is painted an olive green by Little Greene that adds depth and warmth. The Synergy Grace sofa bed by Ormatec is paired with the Firmo bedside cabinet by AM.PM; a vintage wall sconce on the left and an Umbrella floor lamp by Artefact on the right complete the scene. On the wall hangs a work by Ksusha Dmitrieva.

There were certainly some challenges. The original floor plan had issues that required a careful reorganization of the space. “The builder had left a rather bulky load-bearing pillar, as well as two separate small bathrooms. It wasn’t possible to move the column, so we decided to integrate it into the layout, concealing it inside a large closet in the entryway,” explains Chumakova. “This allowed us to create a more functional space while making the structure less noticeable. We also combined the two bathrooms. This created a more comfortable home and also provided additional storage.”

The kitchen was custom-designed by Avantiz and includes elegant vintage pendants.

Another challenge was the lack of natural light. “The apartment receives little direct sunlight, so we paid close attention to the choice of colors and finishes,” says Chumakova. “For example, the light, milk-colored porcelain tile, initially selected for the floor, appeared much more beige in the space than we had expected. We replaced it and also adjusted the other colors to account for this effect, striving to keep the interior as bright and warm as possible.” The lighting design also took into consideration the various needs of daily life, without relying on single light sources. “We planned several different lighting scenarios: integrated ceiling lighting, pendant lamps, accent lights for display cases, and other spotlights,” explains Chumakova. “Throughout the apartment, there are about five different types of lighting.”

To make the best use of space, the design focuses on versatile furniture and solutions that serve multiple purposes, helping to avoid clutter in the rooms. “Instead of the classic light fixture above the dining table, we chose a solution that would maintain the open feeling of the space and not limit the table to a specific position,” says Chumakova. “We also opted for a sofa bed instead of a traditional bed. This allowed us to keep more open space and make the room more versatile: During the day, it can be used as a place to relax and spend time with the family.”

In the kitchen, as in the entryway, the floor is covered with white tiles with black square accents, a graphic detail that picks up the colors of the vintage lighting fixtures.

The layout, however, prioritizes the kitchen, designed to be the heart of this home. “We deliberately wanted to dedicate a significant portion of the apartment to it, because it’s one of the most important areas of the home for its owner,” says Chumakova. “The work area is located near the window overlooking a courtyard: It’s a pleasant spot to spend time cooking or simply looking at the scene outside.”

All the storage systems were custom-designed to blend seamlessly with the interiors. “We wanted the cabinets to be almost invisible,” says Chumakova. “There are two large storage units in the apartment: one in the entryway and one in the living room.”

In the living room, custom cabinets by Avantiz are embellished with retro details. The Dovlet House rug, the vintage table, and the chairs combine contemporary pieces with retro charm.

The color choices help define the different areas without visually fragmenting the space. “We used color to distinguish the entryway from the living room, while the ceiling and the doors of the built-in closet in the entryway were painted the same shade,” Chumakova explains. A natural color palette and materials with distinct textures were chosen to create a warm and inviting atmosphere. “The main colors are olive green in the entryway and a milky white in the sofa bed area,” says Chumakova. “More intense touches of orange appear only in certain details, such as the pouf’s upholstery and the floor lamp. Everything complements the natural walnut, one of the key materials in the interior.”

The entryway features walls painted in an olive green from Little Greene paint and a white tile floor dotted with small black accents. A vintage pendant, and the orange Justyna pouf from La Redoute that adds a vibrant pop of color, complete the look.

In the entryway, the floor is covered with white tiles dotted with small black accents, in keeping with the contemporary classic style. The moldings, found in the entryway and on the wall behind the sofa, add rhythm and depth to the surfaces, giving a modern twist to a traditional detail.

The small size of the apartment required some very careful choices when it came to furnishings. “It was important to find furniture on the right scale: pieces that wouldn’t overwhelm the small apartment and that, at the same time, would be consistent with the contemporary classic aesthetic,” says the interior designer. “So we reinterpreted some traditional elements, from walnut moldings to vintage lamps with distinctive shapes, adapting them to a modern residential complex and the compact dimensions of the apartment.”

In the bath, the wooden cabinet is custom-made by Avantiz; the Evana fixtures by Villeroy & Boch and the Oxford faucet by Migliore complement the vintage sconces.

The finished apartment won over Marta, who oversaw every aspect of the renovation along with her husband. “It was a perfectly successful experiment,” the designer says. “Perhaps, if it had been a more conventional project with the future owner, it would have involved them in every phase and it would have been more challenging. It’s possible that some of the bolder solutions might not have been approved.” In this case, the fact that Marta wanted to surprise her mother also provided greater design freedom, resulting in a space that would be perfectly suited to its future resident.

This article originally appeared in AD Italia.

#5101. Elliptic says Binance hackers swapped stolen tokens for $124M in bitcoin, exchange plans more withdrawal reopenings

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The laundering operation spans 20,000 wallets and 16 blockchains, but Elliptic says little has likely been cashed out.

The attackers in last week's $6.43 billion breach of the Binance crypto exchange have managed to swap around $124 million in altcoins into bitcoin, according to an update from blockchain analysis firm Elliptic.

This is on top of the $1.48 billion in bitcoin stolen in the attack, which is being attributed to North Korea.

Elliptic added that the laundering operation has thus far used more than 20,000 wallets across 16 blockchains and 65,000 transactions, in an attempt to obfuscate the source of the funds. Despite this, the firm estimated that only a "negligible amount" has likely been cashed out.

The largest portion of the converted bitcoin came from stolen XRP swapped through THORChain. Elliptic's report said that by early Friday, roughly $50 million in XRP had been swapped, primarily into bitcoin, with about $226 million remaining.

The report added that nearly all of the stolen Solana (SOL) has already been mixed, swapped, or bridged into bitcoin.

Around $3.11 billion, mostly in ether and bitcoin, remains in addresses linked to the attack, while $2.85 billion of the total  $6.43 billion has been frozen. It's unclear if the $470 million not accounted for here is due to price changes since the attack, is from funds that have already been cashed out, or is a mix of both.

The Block has reached out to the report's author, Tom Robinson, for clarification.

Elliptic wrote that it expects the attackers to swap the rest of the ether for bitcoin in small batches before trying to cash it out through OTC brokers and Chinese money laundering networks.

The firm also rejected the speculation that AI agents were doing the laundering.

"The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," Elliptic said.

On Friday, Binance wrote on X that it was working to reopen withdrawals for HYPE, INJ, APT, PLASMA, NEAR and LTC by the "end of next week." It is also aiming to reopen withdrawals "for all tokens on networks that are already restored," including ETH, BSC, Arbitrum and Optimism.

The exchange's own report, published Tuesday, said the attack was carried out over months and involved a tampered developer tool, a fake job interview and compromised internal AI tooling that got the attackers access to Binance's wallet-signing infrastructure.

Binance has since replenished virtually all the assets stolen in the breach.

#5099. Thailand SEC Files Criminal Complaint Against Bitget Ahead of Dec. 5 Access Block

Thailand's SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget operator BG Limited for unlicensed Thai trading from 2022 to 2025.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission filed a criminal complaint Thursday against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, alleging it ran an unlicensed digital-asset exchange for Thai users from 2022 to 2025, and notified the government to block the platform from Dec. 5.

The complaint, lodged with the Economic Crime Suppression Division, also names Mr. Kritsana Kritsananuwat as an alleged supporter who promoted Bitget through Facebook and YouTube channels, the SEC said in statement No. 241/2026.

From 2022 through 2025, the regulator found, Bitget offered a trading system in the ordinary course of business, charged fees, ran a Thai-language website that could show values in baht, and solicited Thai users on social channels including “Bitget Thailand” on X, “BGB Thai Holder” on Telegram and “bitgetthailand” on TikTok.

That conduct amounts to operating a digital-asset exchange under Section 3 of the Emergency Decree on Digital Asset Businesses B.E. 2561 (2018) without a license, a violation of Section 26 punishable under Section 66, the SEC said. The case now moves to an inquiry official, then prosecutors and the courts.

Separately, the SEC submitted Bitget’s website, other domains and its app to the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society under a 2025 royal decree that authorizes access blocks on unlicensed trading platforms. From Dec. 5, the public “will be unable to access the platform,” the statement said, and investors still holding assets there were told to consider acting before the cutoff.

The enforcement push lands weeks after Bitget announced a planned acquisition of GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics (Thailand), a Bangkok digital-asset exchange and brokerage that already holds Thai licenses, through its interest in BTGT Management (Thailand) Ltd.

Under that deal, GMO Internet and its Thai holding company would transfer their entire stake in GMO-Z — about 49% and 50.94%, respectively — to BTGT or a designee at completion, a Sept. 24 GMO Internet filing showed. Bitget said completion remains subject to regulatory approval and, after a change-of-control nod, it intends to introduce its trading platform to Thai users under local rules.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said in the Oct. 20 release.

The timetable has already slipped. GMO Internet Group pushed the effective date of the share transfer from Oct. 31 to Nov. 30, citing careful work on Thai regulatory applications and closing procedures. That revised date sits five days before the planned Bitget access block.

CoinDesk reported that Bitget’s own terms of use, updated in October, already list Thailand among jurisdictions where its services are prohibited, and that the SEC has not said whether the criminal complaint will weigh on the GMO-Z change of control.

Thailand has used the same access-blocking tool before. In mid-2025 the SEC referred Bybit, OKX and three other unlicensed platforms for a June 28 cutoff, telling users to move assets first. The Bitget action follows that template — and adds a criminal complaint against the exchange operator and a named promoter.

Licensed venues still sit at the center of Bangkok’s policy mix. Thailand has approved a multi-year capital-gains tax break only for trades on SEC-regulated platforms, and has been expanding regulated crypto products in derivatives and capital markets. Unlicensed platforms get the opposite treatment: investor alerts, access blocks and, now for Bitget, a police referral.

What is not yet clear is whether a criminal case against BG Limited for the 2022–2025 period bars, slows or leaves untouched BTGT’s bid to take over a licensed local operator. The SEC statement does not mention GMO-Z. Bitget has not publicly answered the complaint in a statement posted with the acquisition release.

For Thai users still on Bitget, the practical clock is Dec. 5. For Bitget, the test is whether Nov. 30 closes a regulated door into Thailand — or whether the complaint filed Thursday makes that licensed path the next question the ECD and the SEC have to answer.

#5098. Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

"NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here," the project's official account tweeted.

NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here. $ZEC pic.twitter.com/rPaSFe9h7j

"This is a huge milestone for Shielded Labs," Jason McGee of Shielded Labs, the Zcash development organization behind the mechanism, tweeted ahead of the activation.

The NSM tackles a problem Zcash inherited from Bitcoin: whether, as block subsidies decline with each halving, transaction fees alone will be enough to pay miners to secure the network. Under the mechanism, 60% of transaction fees will be removed from circulation and gradually reissued through future block rewards, while preserving the 21 million ZEC supply cap. Reissuance will begin in 2031, with the recycled funds spread along a smooth curve on top of the existing halving schedule.

The NSM was Shielded Labs' first project and builds on an idea that developer Nate Wilcox proposed years ago, originally called the Zcash Posterity Fund, McGee said. A separate proposal under consideration would require Zcash Community Grants to contribute 50,000 ZEC from its treasury to the NSM.

— Jason McGee 🛡 (@aquietinvestor) November 5, 2026

NU7 also limits how much private transaction activity can fit into each block, reducing the data wallets must scan to find their payments, according to the upgrade specification.

Shielded Labs' original design would have replaced Zcash's halvings with a smooth issuance curve. Community panels supported that change, but coinholders did not, so an alternative design that kept the halvings was developed instead.

"We still think the original design was simpler and better, but we respect the process that led us here," McGee wrote. "It should be difficult to change the protocol, and the process should resist capture by any single organization, group, or influential individual."

The upgrade arrives as Zcash's community debates the future of Zcash Community Grants (ZCG) and its share of the Dev Fund, the slice of block rewards that pays for development.

The five organizations that coordinate Zcash's coinholder voting, Project Tachyon, Shielded Labs, Valar Group, the Zcash Foundation and ZODL, have set out an expedited timeline for proposals and polling, saying "tensions were high" and that a drawn-out debate risked "deepening divisions within the Zcash community."

Proposals are due by November 8, with polls for coinholders and community panels running from November 16 to November 30. If rough consensus emerges, the groups will support a further network upgrade, NU 7.1, to implement it.

But thoughtful presents are our speciality if you’re racking your brain for investment ideas that will impress (and not just end up on someone’s eBay account). #5095. Trump administration moves closer to allowing crypto in 401(k) retirement plans

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

This comes months after the DOL unveiled its initial rule outlining steps those 401(k) plan managers should take when considering incorporating alternative assets such as digital assets.

A proposed rule that would open up 401(k) retirement accounts to alternative assets, including cryptocurrencies, has landed at the White House for consideration.

On Thursday, the Department of Labor filed a proposal with the White House's Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs (OIRA), a division within the Office of Management and Budget that reviews federal regulations before publication, to further President Donald Trump's executive order directing the department to pave the way for including alternative assets in 401(k) plans.

"Such clarification must aim to identify the criteria that fiduciaries should use to prudently balance potentially higher expenses against the objectives of seeking greater long-term net returns and broader diversification of investments," the DOL said in its filing.

This comes months after the DOL unveiled its initial rule outlining steps those 401(k) plan managers should take when considering incorporating alternative assets such as digital assets and private equity into their investment profiles. Trump, earlier, had directed the DOL through his executive order.

The DOL also earlier reversed guidance discouraging retirement managers from considering crypto as an investment option in 401(k) plans after the department, under the Biden Administration, had raised concerns about crypto in those retirement plans.

The proposal to allow for crypto in 401(k) plans has garnered scrutiny on Capitol Hill. Earlier this year, Sens. Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren pushed the DOL to scrap the rule and said it would expose investors to volatile assets, including crypto.

In the filing on Thursday, when describing the costs and benefits, the DOL said it was still considering the costs.

#5093. Bitcoin Magazine Books Opens 333-Copy Genesis Book Collector Edition for 3rd Anniversary

Bitcoin Magazine Books opened a third-anniversary campaign Friday for Aaron van Wirdum’s The Genesis Book, offering a redesigned Collectors Edition limited to 333 hand-signed, individually numbered hardcovers in an exclusive Genesis Block slipcase.

Each Collectors Edition hardcover is hand-signed and hand-numbered by van Wirdum, the former Bitcoin Magazine editor-in-chief whose book traces the people, projects and ideas that preceded Satoshi Nakamoto’s peer-to-peer cash design. The standard package is priced at $221 and pairs the book with a limited trading card carrying a Certificate of Authenticity, a collector pin, a sticker sheet, the slipcase and a digital package.

Two one-of-one packages sit outside the ordinary number queue. The #1 package is listed at $5,000 and the #21 package at $2,500; each includes the matching numbered copy, a matching one-of-one collector card and the same accessories as the standard collector package.

Standard collector numbers are assigned in purchase order starting at #2 and skipping #21, the campaign FAQ says. Once preorders close, the signed Genesis print run will not be reproduced.

The Genesis Book Collector's Edition is live!333 copies.Individually numbered.Exclusive Genesis Block slipcase.Hand-signed by Aaron van Wirdum.Once they're gone, they're gone. 🚀 pic.twitter.com/BgiJoCVt8m

Beside the signed run, Bitcoin Magazine Books is selling a Premium Edition hardcover for $160 with foil stamping, a ribbon marker, stained page edges and custom endsheets. That edition is not limited, signed, numbered or slipcased, and it does not include the collector accessories or digital files.

A standalone Digital Package at $9.99 delivers the ebook in EPUB and the audiobook in MP4 immediately after purchase, independent of whether the physical campaign clears its funding bar.

Physical collector and premium orders are scheduled to ship in calendar year 2027.

Store images for the slipcase and accessories are artistic renderings; the campaign page notes that color, materials and finishes may vary in production. The campaign is operated by BTC Inc., which publishes Bitcoin Magazine.

#5092. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

A former FBI agent has pleaded guilty to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency using wallet credentials the bureau had collected during its own investigations, the Justice Department said on Thursday.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on March 4, 2027.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, pled guilty today to wire fraud and money laundering related to using his access to steal cryptocurrency.@FBIWFO https://t.co/jDVtSiAM3H

— U.S. Attorney EDVA (@EDVAnews) November 5, 2026

Yaroch worked at the FBI's Boston Division from 2017 to early 2025 on a squad investigating an unnamed "adversarial nation state," before moving to the bureau's Counterintelligence and Espionage Division, where he had access to FBI systems holding crypto credentials gathered in investigations, according to prosecutors.

Beginning no later than December 2024, he used that access to steal crypto from wallets controlled by subjects of other investigations, by nation-state actors, and from at least one wallet the FBI had created for an operation. He made about 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials from 35 separate FBI investigations, then swapped the stolen funds into other cryptocurrencies and moved them through further transactions to hide their origin. He used some of the money to pay off credit cards, car payments and other loans.

Yaroch also planned a new life abroad. He asked ChatGPT how to maximize profits from investments and where to live in the European Union, and after the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to obtain a tax identification number for him, prosecutors said. He arranged to travel to Portugal in September.

His actions compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, as well as sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection, according to the plea agreement's statement of facts.

Yaroch turned himself in. On July 29, he broke down while telling a former colleague at the Justice Department's National Security Division that he had made "very poor decisions" involving crypto wallets, according to an FBI affidavit filed in the case. He then self-reported to FBI headquarters, saying he had become frustrated that the bureau "could not or would not act" against an adversary's crypto accounts and had decided to "take matters into his own hands."

At the time, Yaroch said he had made about 10 to 12 transfers worth under $1 million, far less than the figures in his plea. Agents searching his home two days later seized a Trezor hardware wallet, three passports and a slip of paper bearing seed phrases, according to the affidavit. His Kraken account held about $188,600, mostly in U.S. dollars and the USDC stablecoin, with smaller amounts in Squid (QUID) and Bitcoin. A further $933,800 sat in Suilend, a lending protocol on the Sui blockchain, which he had accessed through the Slush wallet app. Yaroch told agents he chose Suilend "simply because he liked that the logo was a water droplet."

Agents transferred about $925,000 from those accounts to government-controlled wallets, the affidavit said. The roughly $165,600 held as U.S. dollars on Kraken could not be moved the same way.

Decrypt reported on the charges against Yaroch in August.

#5090. Gnosis Pay data breach exposes customer info; funds unaffected

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach after unauthorized access to its legacy debit card system on Nov. 3. Funds were unaffected; 3D Secure is now mandatory.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach affecting its legacy system powering Gnosis Pay-branded debit cards, exposing certain customer information after unauthorized access was detected on Nov. 3.

The company said it contained the incident within two hours, replaced compromised security credentials and addressed the vulnerability, adding that customer funds were not affected and its enterprise platform powering white-label card programs was untouched.

Gnosis Pay has made 3D Secure verification mandatory for online card payments until the affected system is retired on Dec. 20.

Crypto card providers increasingly rely on third-party infrastructure and legacy systems that can create security vulnerabilities. In recent months, there have been breaches exposing cardholder information and exploits affecting funds held in card accounts.

Based said an attacker accessed an internal dashboard used to manage its Visa card program on Oct. 5. Some customer data was exposed, but the attacker reportedly failed to access deposited funds. In August, a vulnerability in Rain's Solana card infrastructure allowed unauthorized withdrawals from card balances belonging to users of Tria and Avici.

Meanwhile, Revolut said an unauthorized third party used a fraudulent request from a legitimate government email domain to obtain sensitive customer information, potentially including account statements, identity documents and Bitcoin transaction histories. Customer funds and systems were unaffected.
